Self-Check: Answer

  1. A cluster monitoring dashboard reports \(85\%\) GPU utilization, yet researchers complain of slow progress and low experiment output. What distinction between fleet utilization metrics explains this apparent contradiction?

    1. Allocated utilization (GPUs reserved by containers) may be high (\(85\%\)) while compute utilization (SM active time) is low (\(20\%\)) due to data loader stalls, or productive utilization (goodput advancing converged training steps) is near zero due to unhandled AllReduce synchronization deadlocks.
    2. Hardware sensors on GPUs report utilization in hexadecimal rather than decimal percentages.
    3. Grafana dashboards invert metric percentages when cluster temperature exceeds 25 degrees Celsius.
    4. GPU utilization metrics only count CPU core cycles, concealing whether the GPU is plugged into power.

    Answer: The correct answer is A. Cluster observability requires distinguishing between three metrics: (1) Allocated Utilization (fraction of GPUs assigned to jobs, which can be 85%), (2) Compute Utilization (fraction of time GPU SMs are actively executing instructions rather than waiting on CPU data loading, host memory, or I/O), and (3) Productive Utilization / Goodput (fraction of compute advancing valid training steps rather than stalled in communication barriers, deadlocks, or throwaway restarts). The hexadecimal, temperature inversion, and CPU-only options are absurd distractors.

    Learning Objective: Differentiate between Allocated Utilization, Compute Utilization, and Productive Goodput in cluster observability.

  2. What is a ‘preemption cascade’ in a multi-tenant GPU cluster, and how can orchestrators mitigate its destructive impact on cluster throughput?

    1. A preemption cascade occurs when a single GPU catches fire and burns adjacent GPUs in the same server rack; it is mitigated by Halon fire suppression systems.
    2. A preemption cascade occurs when Slurm automatically deletes user source code repositories upon job completion; it is mitigated by Git branch protection.
    3. A preemption cascade occurs when Kubernetes continuously restarts healthy pods due to Docker daemon upgrades; it is mitigated by disabling Linux kernel updates.
    4. A preemption cascade occurs when high-priority jobs evict medium-priority jobs, which in turn evict low-priority jobs across the cluster, triggering simultaneous checkpoint I/O storms and thrashing compute progress; it is mitigated by preemption cooldown periods and holistic preemption limits.

    Answer: The correct answer is D. In multi-priority environments, an influx of high-priority jobs can evict medium-priority jobs, which then preempt low-priority jobs to find room. If dozens of jobs simultaneously start saving checkpoints before termination, the resulting I/O storm saturates shared storage, prolongs eviction delays, and causes cluster thrashing. Schedulers mitigate this using preemption cooldown timers (preventing a recently started job from immediate re-preemption), holistic bandwidth reservation for checkpointing, and non-preempting priority tiers (preemptionPolicy: Never). The physical fire, code deletion, and Docker upgrade options are incorrect.

    Learning Objective: Evaluate the risks of cascading preemption storms and design mechanisms to prevent them.

  3. In a multi-tenant cluster, how does decoupling ‘guaranteed quota’ from ‘limit (burst) quota’ prevent stranded idle capacity while protecting team allocations?

    Answer: A guaranteed quota represents a team’s baseline dedicated capacity that is immediately available and immune to eviction by peers. A limit (burst) quota allows a team to opportunistically utilize idle cluster capacity above their guarantee when overall demand is low. If the owning team reclaims their guaranteed quota, the scheduler preempts the bursting team’s opportunistic jobs, maximizing aggregate utilization without violating baseline access rights.

    Learning Objective: Explain the architectural difference between guaranteed quota and limit quota in shared GPU clusters.

  4. What is ‘quota hoarding’ in shared GPU clusters, and what automated scheduler policies prevent researchers from engaging in it?

    Answer: Quota hoarding occurs when users submit placeholder or dummy sleeping jobs (e.g., interactive shells or infinite loops) to keep GPUs continuously reserved under their quota even when not running useful computation. Schedulers counter this via: (1) Inactivity timeouts that detect zero SM/memory activity and auto-terminate idle sessions; (2) Dynamic lease-based allocations that require active renewal; and (3) Fair-share decay accounting that charges users for holding time regardless of compute intensity.

    Learning Objective: Analyze the organizational anti-pattern of quota hoarding and describe governance mechanisms to eliminate it.

  5. True or False: Under Dominant Resource Fairness (DRF), if Team A’s workload requires \(4\text{ GPUs}\) and \(8\text{ CPU cores}\) per worker while Team B’s workload requires \(1\text{ GPU}\) and \(64\text{ CPU cores}\) per worker on a cluster with \(100\text{ GPUs}\) and \(1000\text{ CPU cores}\), DRF equalizes each team’s share of their dominant resource (GPUs for Team A, CPUs for Team B).

    Answer: True. Dominant Resource Fairness (DRF) multi-resource allocation identifies each tenant’s dominant resource (the resource type for which the tenant requests the largest fraction of total cluster capacity) and equalizes the dominant shares across all active tenants, ensuring multi-resource equity without allowing single-resource monopolies.

    Learning Objective: Apply Dominant Resource Fairness (DRF) principles to multi-resource ML allocations.

  6. **Order the hierarchy of checks performed by a multi-tenant scheduler when Team A in Department X submits a new 16-GPU job request:

  1. Evaluate Team A’s internal user priority and historical fair-share usage decay relative to teammate submissions.
  2. Determine if cluster-wide unallocated capacity or preemption candidates exist to host the 16-GPU allocation.
  3. Verify that Department X has not exceeded its total department-level GPU budget limit.
  4. Check whether Team A’s currently active allocations are within its guaranteed quota or if it must request burst/borrowed capacity.
  5. Grant the lease, bind the containers to physical hardware, and update accounting meters for Department X and Team A.**

Answer: The correct order is (3) -> (4) -> (1) -> (2) -> (5). Explanation: Step (3): Verify that Department X has not exceeded its total department-level GPU budget limit. Step (4): Check whether Team A’s currently active allocations are within its guaranteed quota or if it must request burst/borrowed capacity. Step (1): Evaluate Team A’s internal user priority and historical fair-share usage decay relative to teammate submissions. Step (2): Determine if cluster-wide unallocated capacity or preemption candidates exist to host the 16-GPU allocation. Step (5): Grant the lease, bind the containers to physical hardware, and update accounting meters for Department X and Team A.

Learning Objective: Order the decision hierarchy in a hierarchical multi-tenant fair-share scheduling engine.

← Back to Questions

Self-Check: Answer

  1. An administrator observes that average GPU compute utilization across a 1,000-GPU cluster is only \(55\%\), yet users experience multi-day queue wait times for 64-GPU jobs. Which diagnostic finding points to the primary root cause?

    1. The cluster’s Ethernet cables are physically too long, causing optical signals to decay into radio waves.
    2. Severe resource fragmentation and strict gang scheduling constraints prevent 64-GPU contiguous blocks from forming, while short jobs hold scattered GPUs across nodes without filling them.
    3. The NVIDIA driver automatically caps utilization at 55% when GPU fans are running on low speed.
    4. Python code running in user containers automatically halts execution whenever cluster utilization drops below 60%.

    Answer: The correct answer is B. In multi-tenant GPU clusters, moderate average utilization (e.g., 55%) frequently coexists with long queue times due to fragmentation and gang scheduling requirements: uncoordinated, non-gang small jobs occupy 1-2 GPUs per node across many nodes, leaving hundreds of GPUs idle in aggregate but preventing any single 64-GPU contiguous gang allocation from assembling. Cable length, fan speed capping, and Python auto-halting are fictional distractors.

    Learning Objective: Analyze the root causes of high queue wait times coexisting with moderate average cluster utilization.

  2. During a multi-GPU training job, DCGM reports that GPU SM utilization oscillates rapidly between \(10\%\) and \(95\%\) with low memory copy throughput, while top on the host node shows \(100\%\) CPU utilization across all cores and high disk wait (\(iowait\)). What is the root cause and remedy?

    1. The GPU hardware is defective; the operator should replace the GPUs with CPU-only nodes.
    2. The PyTorch model has too many layers; the model architecture must be pruned to 1 layer.
    3. The training job is bottlenecked on host data loading, decompression, or augmentation; the remedy is optimizing PyTorch num_workers, utilizing shared memory (/dev/shm), caching preprocessed tensors on local NVMe SSDs, or offloading transforms to GPU using DALI.
    4. The cluster InfiniBand switch is operating in half-duplex mode; the administrator must power cycle the spine switch.

    Answer: The correct answer is C. An oscillating GPU utilization pattern accompanied by pinned host CPU cores and high \(iowait\) is the classic signature of an input data pipeline bottleneck: GPUs finish backpropagation rapidly, then stall waiting for the host CPU DataLoader workers to fetch, decompress, and augment the next batch of training images or tokens. Remedies include increasing/tuning num_workers, storing datasets on local NVMe SSDs, pinning host memory (pin_memory=True), using /dev/shm, or leveraging GPU-accelerated pipelines like NVIDIA DALI. The other options are incorrect diagnoses and destructive actions.

    Learning Objective: Identify hardware telemetry patterns indicating host CPU and I/O bottlenecks in GPU training runs.

  3. What is a ‘zombie allocation’ (or ghost job) in a GPU cluster, and how can automated health monitors detect and remediate it?

    Answer: A zombie allocation occurs when the scheduler records a GPU as allocated to a running job, but the underlying container or user training process has crashed or entered an unrecoverable CUDA error state, leaving the GPU idle and inaccessible to other users. Automated monitors detect zombies by correlating scheduler allocation tables with DCGM metrics (zero SM and memory activity over a prolonged window) or process tree inspections (no active PID attached to /dev/nvidia*), automatically draining the allocation, killing orphaned processes, and resetting the GPU context.

    Learning Objective: Formulate a diagnostic procedure to identify and clean up zombie GPU allocations.

  4. True or False: In a ring AllReduce across 32 nodes, if 31 nodes show \(95\%\) GPU utilization and 1 node shows \(10\%\) utilization, the node with \(10\%\) utilization is the only healthy node while the 31 nodes are encountering network hardware faults.

    Answer: False. The reverse is true: in synchronous ring AllReduce, a single slow worker or degraded network link (the straggler) slows down the entire collective; while the straggler takes longer to compute or transfer its chunk, all other 31 workers finish their compute early and spin in the synchronization barrier (often registering high kernel active time in spin-locks or waiting for the straggler to complete). Profiling communication timelines reveals the straggler as the source of the delay.

    Learning Objective: Evaluate telemetry signals to pinpoint straggler nodes in multi-node AllReduce rings.

  5. When diagnosing training performance bottlenecks, operators inspect the ____ throughput (measured in GB/s) to ensure that batches transferred from host memory to accelerator HBM are not constrained by host bus contention.

    Answer: PCIe (or PCIe bandwidth, PCIe throughput, PCIe transfer). PCIe transfer bandwidth (Host-to-Device / H2D) is a key diagnostic metric for detecting unpinned memory copies and bus saturation during data loading.

    Learning Objective: Identify the hardware metric used to diagnose host-to-device data transfer bottlenecks.

← Back to Questions

Self-Check: Answer

  1. Why is targeting near \(100\%\) GPU cluster utilization an operational anti-pattern for an interactive AI research and development organization?

    1. GPU hardware components melt if utilization exceeds \(90\%\) for more than 5 consecutive minutes.
    2. Modern deep learning frameworks automatically downgrade model accuracy when cluster utilization exceeds \(85\%\).
    3. Operating above \(95\%\) utilization causes cloud providers to double their hourly billing rates without warning.
    4. As utilization approaches \(100\%\), queuing theory (Pollaczek-Khinchine) dictates that job queue wait times explode hyperbolically, delaying fast feedback for exploratory experiments and severely reducing overall engineering velocity.

    Answer: The correct answer is D. According to queuing theory, as cluster utilization (\(\rho\)) approaches \(1.0\), the waiting time in the queue diverges toward infinity (\(W_q \propto \frac{\rho}{1-\rho}\)). In research organizations with heavy-tailed job sizes (\(C_s \approx 3\)), operating above \(80-85\%\) utilization causes small debugging and exploratory jobs to wait days in queues. This destroys developer velocity and feedback loops, proving that maximizing raw utilization at the expense of queue latency is an anti-pattern. Hardware melting, accuracy downgrading, and stealth billing rate doubling are fabricated.

    Learning Objective: Evaluate why operating a research GPU cluster at near 100% utilization is counterproductive to organizational velocity.

  2. Explain the fallacy that ‘more sophisticated scheduling algorithms always improve utilization,’ and describe what operators should audit before replacing their cluster scheduler.

    Answer: Low cluster utilization and long wait times are more frequently caused by policy misconfigurations than algorithmic limitations. Before replacing or upgrading a scheduler, operators should audit: (1) Resource request inflation (users requesting 8 GPUs when 1 is needed); (2) Unconfigured gang scheduling timeouts; (3) Mismatched hardware partitions (e.g., jobs requiring A100s when A10s suffice); and (4) Stale fair-share decay parameters that fail to balance contention.

    Learning Objective: Explain why policy misconfigurations rather than scheduling algorithm limitations are often the root cause of poor cluster efficiency.

  3. True or False: Gang scheduling is strictly mandatory for every distributed ML workload, including asynchronous parameter-server training, hyperparameter sweeps, and elastic fine-tuning jobs.

    Answer: False. Gang scheduling is mandatory only for bulk-synchronous parallel training where every worker must reach every AllReduce barrier. Workloads using asynchronous gradient updates (e.g., parameter servers), independent hyperparameter trials, or elastic training frameworks with dynamic worker membership can make useful progress with variable or partial allocations, and enforcing rigid gang scheduling on them unnecessarily increases queue wait times.

    Learning Objective: Evaluate when gang scheduling constraints can be relaxed in distributed machine learning workloads.

← Back to Questions

Self-Check: Answer

  1. Which statement best synthesizes the central architectural challenge of fleet orchestration for machine learning?

    1. Orchestration is purely a software compilation task that translates PyTorch code into C++ CUDA binaries without considering cluster hardware.
    2. Orchestration requires balancing physical hardware constraints (NVLink topology, failure rates, power limits) against organizational policies (fairness, priorities, cost) across diverse, long-running, and failure-prone distributed workloads.
    3. Orchestration has been completely solved by standard web application load balancers like NGINX, which handle GPU AllReduce synchronization transparently.
    4. Orchestration only matters for single-GPU laptops and has no relevance to multi-thousand GPU datacenters.

    Answer: The correct answer is B. Fleet orchestration operates at the intersection of physical infrastructure (GPU heterogeneity, NVLink topologies, power boundaries, component failure rates) and organizational objectives (fair-share equity, priority preemption, budget optimization, latency vs. throughput). It translates multi-dimensional, distributed resource demands into physical allocations while ensuring high goodput and fault resilience. Software compilation, NGINX load balancing, and laptop-only claims misrepresent the role of orchestration.

    Learning Objective: Synthesize the primary architectural trade-offs across the fleet orchestration stack.

  2. How is the historical boundary between HPC batch scheduling (Slurm) and cloud-native service orchestration (Kubernetes) evolving to meet the demands of modern ML lifecycles?

    Answer: The boundary is converging: Kubernetes is integrating HPC capabilities (gang scheduling via Volcano/Kueue, topology-aware placement, RDMA bypass, and fair-share queues) to support large-scale distributed training, while HPC environments are incorporating containerization, declarative APIs, and dynamic service management. Organizations increasingly deploy hybrid architectures where outer Kubernetes control planes manage end-to-end pipelines and serving, orchestrating high-performance batch training clusters beneath.

    Learning Objective: Explain how the convergence of cloud-native and HPC orchestration paradigms addresses modern AI training and serving needs.

  3. True or False: In large-scale ML cluster management, maximizing raw GPU allocated utilization (the percentage of GPUs assigned to active containers) guarantees optimal economic value and research productivity.

    Answer: False. Raw allocated utilization measures only resource reservation, not productive work. A cluster with \(95\%\) allocated utilization may deliver near-zero research value if GPUs are stalled in AllReduce deadlocks, starved by slow CPU data loaders, thrashing in preemption cascades, or executing diverged training runs. Productive Goodput—the rate of valid, converged training steps completed per dollar spent—is the definitive measure of fleet efficiency.

    Learning Objective: Justify why Productive Goodput rather than raw GPU utilization is the definitive metric for fleet efficiency.
